ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e303	No one has asked for the floor.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e317	I would like to thank you for the opportunity to explain the status and management of the outbreak of the coronary virus. It has now been almost a year since we introduced the most radical measures in peacetime. Over this year, more than 4 million tests have been carried out, over 75 000 have been detected, 3,400 have been hospitalized, and 632 deaths have been reported. Many are affected by the virus, and more are affected by the fight against the infection. But compared to most other countries, we have now come out better. After we got control of the infection on late winter last year, we have managed to keep control, even if the infection has gone into waves. But the epidemic is unstable. Early in January, we tightened up, both at the border and by domestic measures. It gave results, and the infection fell through January. It has also likely helped to delay the spread of the more contagious virus variants in Norway, compared to other countries. In the last few weeks, the numbers have been pointing up again, and the more contagious variants are taking over as the dominant virus variant. The R's are now estimated to be 1.3. So it's too high. There have been a number of outbreaks around the country. We have seen outbreaks in, among others. Ulvik, Bodø, Harstad, Kristiansand and Oslo. We have seen outbreaks, among others. in hospitals, sports, workplaces, schools and kindergartens. In recent times, most outbreaks have been caused by the more contagious virus variant. Although the infection has increased in eight out of ten counties, some counties dominate. The last few weeks have about 70 pst. of the cases registered in Oslo and Viken. The mutated virus variant is estimated to be around 70 pst. of the infection in these areas. It has greater spread potential and is likely to give rise to a greater risk of severe progression and, consequently, of multiple hospitalisations. At the same time, we know that we have vaccines that also protect against it, and we know that it is possible to also shut it down with known measures. The infection has increased in all age groups. But it's the younger age groups that dominate. The highest rate of increase is between 13 and 19 years of age. Then the group follows 20-39 years. The rise in infection among younger age groups is not unnatural. It's for these groups we've opened up the most. Another number that fortunately increases is the number of people who have been vaccinated. There are now more than 600,000 doses of vaccine. Although it is reassuring to know that the elderly are well protected by vaccination, there are still too many who may be seriously ill with the coronary virus if the infection spreads uncontrolled. Younger age groups may also be seriously ill. Which of us has an underlying disease is not always as easy to know. We therefore see that the increase in infection leads to hospitalisation. The number of hospital inmates is back at the levels we saw in January. In particular, the hospitals in and around Oslo have experienced growth. The increase in infection is also seen in many other countries. Several countries are now introducing strong measures to avoid a powerful third wave. The new virus variants are becoming dominant, making it more demanding to keep the infection under control. It has therefore been necessary to introduce more in-depth measures in many municipalities. We have seen that many municipalities have managed to shut down the outbreaks of mutated viruses, but the situation is fragile. It's essential that we be able to keep control. If we succeed in keeping the infection low also in March and April, we can start to open up in May. We also have to deal with this counter-ground. There is a division of labour between the State and local authorities in the management of infection. The Code empowers both the municipalities and the government to adopt the necessary and proportionate infectious control measures to deal with the pandemic. Throughout the pandemic, we have had national measures, including: through requirements for establishments and limitations on how many can be grouped. National measures have always formed a floor. No municipality can have a lower level. In meeting the more contagious virus variants, it is important that all municipalities support and ensure good enforcement of national measures. But they can have a higher level. In the case of local outbreaks, more invasive local measures are often needed. The starting point has been for municipalities to adopt more stringent measures themselves. The government may also adopt measures in force in parts of the country, but this is where it is necessary to address the situation. This may be the case in an unbridled situation with a major outbreak when rapid coordination is needed between several municipalities. In the event of an outbreak, the Heads of State or Government, the municipalities concerned, the Institute of Public Health and the Directorate of Health cooperate to assess the need for action. They also consider who should decide the local measures: either the individual municipalities themselves or the government. The Government adopted, for the first time, reinforced local regional measures in the context of the Northern Follo outbreak in January. Later, the government has also adopted measures for municipalities, among others. in the West and in Westfold and Telemark. National measures have been replaced by municipal regulation when the infective situation is clarified. To date, all municipalities have agreed, or even wanted, that the government should adopt the measures and make adjustments to them in the situations in which this scheme has been used. The Government has prepared three regulatory action levels that can be introduced very quickly when the need arises: Action Level A, Action Level B and Action Level C. Action Level A has the strictest measures, while Action Level C has the least stringent measures. We listened to the criticism that the first version of the system was too complicated, and we have simplified and improved the system. We have good experience with the regional measures. There has been good cooperation with the relevant municipalities, the Public Health Institute, the Health Directorate and the State administrators. In line with advice from the Health Directorate and the Public Health Institute, the government wants the locally adopted measures to work for a few days before drawing the final conclusion as to whether they are sufficient to bring the infection down. Local measures are well targeted and have a high level of legitimacy. But it is absolutely essential that they are strong enough, are well coordinated, and that they come quickly enough to shut down the outbreaks. The government will therefore strengthen local management at two points. There are some examples that it takes a little too long for municipalities to implement adequate measures and coordinate themselves. The Institute for Public Health has therefore made clear its guidance to municipalities on how to proceed in order to implement measures quickly, coordinated and with an adequate level of action. The urgent competence of the local authority to take the necessary measures is of great importance in such situations. The necessary measures can then be taken quickly by the local doctor, usually coordinated with colleagues in neighbouring municipalities. At the same time, the elected representatives of municipalities will have time to deal with what will be the more sustainable level of action. That way, you don't waste time. The testing and screening of the pathogens may take place before the infection can spread. In this way, the virus can be effectively shut down without long-term intervention. In the future, it must also be the main model for local handling. What the other government will do is to lower the threshold for when it will be relevant for the government to decide on the level of action on behalf of the municipalities. It will apply: where the urgency requires rapid and coordinated regulation, or where the decision-making processes in the municipalities take too long; where municipalities with relatively similar needs in the same region want different measures where there is doubt as to whether the level of action in an area is sufficient to reduce the infection: or if other national considerations so warrant, e.g.: the capacity of the specialist health service; Regional measures adopted by the government may therefore be relevant if infection continues to increase in those parts of the country where there is already a high level of infection. Municipalities will always have the opportunity to take decisions first. That is also what the government wants. If it becomes relevant to intervene, it will be in dialogue with the municipality, and the goal will always be that the municipality itself will get the control back as soon as it is sound. However, we are now alerting this, because we are totally dependent on the effectiveness of local measures if we are to avoid new and stringent national measures. The government’s long-term strategy outlines the choice of targets and measures for the government’s management of the covid-19 pandemic in Norway. Furthermore, we have the contingency plan which provides overall guidance for assessments and decisions of actions at national, regional and local level. We take these into account when assessing measures, and this contributes to a certain degree of predictability, even in situations of more unpredictable infection. We're now dependent on getting the spread down. An R of 1.3 over time will quickly lead to a loss of control. Calculations from the Health Directorate show that if the spread of infection continues at this level, in 75 days, at the end of May, we will be able to have 50 times more daily infections than we have today. Long before we get there, the local authorities' infection tracking system will collapse, and hospitals will have major problems providing healthcare to those who need it. The Health Directorate and the Public Health Institute advise us to follow the situation from day to day in weeks 10 and 11 to see if the relatively strict measures we have maintained nationally since January, in combination with reinforced local measures where there is an increase in infection, are sufficient to reduce the infection again. If this is not the case, the government is prepared to strengthen its national action on short notice. In order for society to be prepared for this, we have asked the Health Directorate and the Public Health Institute to propose which reinforced measures should then be introduced. It is recommended that one should keep a distance of 2 metres from others and not 1 metre from today. A maximum of ten private contacts per week are recommended. For events, the number limits will be changed to 20 persons indoors with fixed assigned seating positions, for funerals the limit will be 50. The same will be true of events for those under 20 years of age, who gather only people from the same municipality. For outdoor events, the limit becomes 50 persons. Prohibitions will be introduced on indoor, organised sports and leisure activities for adults, but not for top professional athletes. Training centres and swimming pools will be closed, with the exception of: what is needed for swimming training and rehabilitation. Amusement parks and bingo halls are also closed. There will be a tipping-off across the country. It will also be stipulated that the employer should ensure that employees work from home as far as practicable. Furthermore, if this is not sufficient to reduce the spread of the new virus variant, the government will consider reintroducing the recommendations of January, including the recommendation not to have visited home for a period of two weeks, postponing all events, red levels of secondary and secondary education and digital education in universities, colleges and colleges. Although we are not introducing new national measures today, I would like to recall the recommendations that apply. Everyone, across the country, has to limit social contact. Should a meeting someone outside the household, do it preferably outdoors. Do not travel abroad. Unnecessary journeys should be avoided domestically. Anyone with a chance should have a home office. Events gathering people from several municipalities should be cancelled or postponed. If all follow these recommendations, it should be sufficient to keep the infection down. We would also recommend that those who are infected and do not have an appropriate place to isolate themselves, contact the municipality – and they can be placed in hotels. As far as Easter measures are concerned, the government comes back with concrete recommendations. How strict they become will depend on the development of infection in the near future. But we're already going to encourage Norwegians living abroad not to come home at Easter this time. It also applies to students. We also encourage the Nordics and people living in Norway not to leave the country at Easter. However, if anyone chooses to leave, they must be prepared for the government to consider extending the quarantine accommodation scheme to new groups. This means that if you are travelling abroad on a leisure trip now, you will probably have to take the quarantine at the hotel when you return home. So the government's advice to avoid all travel abroad that is not strictly necessary is still valid and is valid until 15 April. We will also consider laying down the requirement for separate bedrooms and private baths and that you must be able to stay at least two metres away from other household members. This means that one can risk fines for physical conditions that do not facilitate good quarantine in one's own home. We also recommend that digital education in universities and colleges be planned for the first week after Easter. The government prioritises children and young people. This means that children and young people should have the lowest possible response burden. We therefore want kindergarten and school life to be as normal as possible. At the same time, we must ensure a good level of infection protection adapted to the new virus variants. It's a demanding task, but it's absolutely necessary. Protecting children and young people from invasive infectious diseases also has a negative impact. We have seen an increase in infection among children and young people in recent times and many outbreaks in schools and kindergartens. It's worrying. Increased infection among children and young people calls for more stringent measures affecting others. But the government believes it is right to protect children and young people as far as possible. In Norway, we have largely managed to keep schools and kindergartens open since the spring of last year, unlike many other countries. We have made this clear, among other things. because we have created different levels of intervention for schools and kindergartens. We've got a lot of reports from kindergartens and schools that they want an update of the supervisors after the new virus variants have arrived. We have also received signals that today’s responses vary and that there are practical challenges to today’s responses. Some areas have experienced a rapid return to a red level, such as the United Kingdom and the United Kingdom. in Oslo. There is therefore reason to consider whether action levels are well adapted to a more infectious virus and the actual conditions in kindergarten and school. Now we're taking new steps to ensure the best possible combination of infectious disease protection and presence. We depend on playing teams with local authorities. So we have to involve the municipal sector in what we do. Firstly, we have entrusted the Directorate of Health, the Institute of Public Health and the Directorate of Education with the task of reviewing the infectious agents. The agencies are engaged in this process in dialogue with municipalities and the parties to the sector. Secondly, this week we will send letters to municipalities, county councils and other kindergarten and school owners on measures to ensure that children and young people can be more at school. Thirdly, we reiterate the assurance that municipalities will be compensated for additional costs of infectious diseases and for measures they are taking to ensure that the protection of infection is ensured and that as much as possible can be involved in physical education. We also stress that it is the responsibility of the municipalities that the money goes all the way to the individual kindergarten and school and thus ensures the situation for students and employees in the school. With good guidance and good facilities at the level of measures, we can better ensure the protection of infectious diseases while at the same time helping to maximise the presence in kindergarten and school, thus keeping the burden of action low for children and young people. Recreational restrictions have been an important part of the government’s management of the pandemic since March 2020. Norway is one of the countries in Europe with the highest levels of entry regulations and quarantine rules throughout the pandemic. When the United Kingdom announced the mutated virus variant, the government quickly established specific entry and testing rules for travellers from there. In addition, at the end of January, the government further tightened the access of non-residents to entry into Norway. In principle, only foreigners residing in Norway are now allowed to enter. Derogations are still made for groups deemed to have a particular connection with Norway or an important purpose of their visit, including close family members, non-residents in critical social functions, non-residents in the transport sector and health professionals from Sweden and Finland. The duration and details of the restrictions on entry are continuously assessed in line with the development of infection and up to alternative and less invasive measures. It is important that these restrictions do not apply beyond what is necessary. The strict restrictions on entry imposed from 29 January were necessary to limit the risk of contamination of imports into Norway. In order to limit imports, the measure has been effective. Import infection rates are now low, although infection in Norway is increasing. At the same time, restrictions have led to a very difficult situation for some workers working in Norway and living in other countries. They have not gotten to work, and they have lost their income. From midnight on Monday 1 March, we eased the restrictions by opening up the possibility for day commuters from Sweden and Finland to return to work in Norway under a strict testing and control regime. At the same time, many are still subject to the ban, and not least now that restrictions on entry seem to be able to continue for a while. Many of these have earned rights in popular fear. In view of this, the government considers it necessary to provide an alternative income hedge to those affected. The Government proposes a new compensation scheme based on the sickness pay scheme. It makes it possible to set up a system relatively quickly. The performance shall cover 70 pst. on the basis of sickness benefit, which is approximately the same compensation rate as the worker would have received if they were covered by the temporary leave rules. The government proposes that employers be responsible for the advance of benefits and then receive reimbursement from the social security of the entire amount. It shall apply from 29 January at the time of the introduction of the entry ban and shall be granted for the duration of the ban on workers. The government will quickly return to the Stortinget with proposals for legislation and a detailed description of the scheme. Recreational restrictions also have a significant impact on Norwegian enterprises that rely on foreign labour, and restrictions on entry may have an impact on the development of the Norwegian economy. These impacts are likely to increase if the restrictions on entry last for a period of time. The government therefore launched on 20 February an application-based scheme for exemptions from the restrictions on the entry of workers not already covered by existing derogations. The scheme will not address the business needs of people for normal work. The risk of opening up so many is too big. The scheme initially applies to a limited group of workers considered as strictly necessary to avoid cessation of activity. These are persons with technical competence who carry out work related to machinery and technical equipment and who have a working or professional relationship with the applicant undertaking. The scheme is managed by the Directorate-General for Maritime Affairs. They deserve a big thank you for taking on a whole new task quickly. By 5 March, it had been adopted in excess of 1000 decisions in the scheme, including some 500 decisions on granted entry. The government is now considering some extensions of the scheme to include other groups of workers after an overall assessment of the infectiveness situation and business needs. The main priority of the government in the work on vaccines is early vaccination of the entire population. Due to limited access initially, we have prioritised hospital residents and the elderly with the greatest risk of dying of covid-19. There are errors when it is produced as if Norway were located after other European countries in terms of vaccination. As you know, we are covered by the EU vaccine strategy, and we are allocated doses of the same key as the other countries. At the moment, statistics show that Norway is among the leading European countries to vaccinate according to the number of doses allocated. The groups 75 to 84 years of age and healthcare professionals are now vaccinated. We've set over 600 000 doses. The Public Health Institute has recommended a modest geographical redistribution of vaccines to Oslo, Fredrikstad, Sarpsborg, Moss and Lørenskog. It is believed that Oslo prioritises the districts of Stovner, Alna, Grorud, Bjerke, Søndre Nordstrand and Old Oslo, which have had the highest infection pressure over time. At the same time, a number of other changes are being made which will have a much greater impact on the distribution and vaccination rate in municipalities. Firstly, the Swedish Institute of Public Health is moving from distributing the vaccines according to the number of inhabitants who have over 65 years of age to a distribution according to the number of inhabitants they have over 18 years of age. This means that municipalities with a younger population will receive a higher number of doses. Secondly, the AstraZeneca vaccine is now also recommended for people over 65 years of age. It is based on new knowledge of the efficacy of vaccines. This means that many in the top priority groups can be vaccinated two to five weeks earlier than expected. After professional input from the Institute of Public Health, the government has decided that the interval between dose one and two from the Pfizer-BioNTech vaccine can be extended from three to six weeks. The Institute of Public Health will come to a final professional conclusion in a short time. The Public Health Institute will also assess whether the interval should be extended beyond six weeks. With a longer vaccine interval, several may receive the first dose earlier. The Public Health Institute also looks at whether the interval can be further extended. The most important thing is that vaccine access is increasing. Now we receive about 100,000 vaccine doses a week. At the end of March, we expect about twice as much. In April, May and June, deliveries increase even more. We therefore expect a significant increase in the number of vaccine doses in the next few months. That gives hope. Probably new vaccines are coming, too. The product authorities started the authorisation process for CureVac and Novavax in February. On 11 March, the Janssen vaccine may be approved. This is a one-dose vaccine that will help to further increase the vaccination rate beyond springtime. We are now expecting all adults to have received a vaccine offer within or during the summer. A few European countries are impatient and will go their own way to get more vaccines now and in the future that can protect against new virus variants. All approved vaccines available are currently used and it is therefore not possible to acquire multiple doses in the short term of this type of vaccine. Norway, together with other countries, also builds a readiness for existing vaccines to lose their power against the new mutated viruses. Therefore, through EU cooperation, we are now concluding agreements on future vaccine deliveries for vaccines that can be adjusted to combat new mutations. We've also invested 200 million. kr in the vaccine cooperative CEPI to intensify the development of vaccines against future mutations of the virus. Since March last year, we have said that we should have financial measures for as long as we have infectious diseases and that these will be adapted to the disease situation and the level of infectious diseases. As recently as two weeks ago, the Stortinget discussed government proposals for the extension and adjustment of a number of support schemes in response to the pandemic. The background to the proposals was inter alia: the evolution of the infectiveness situation and the uncertainty about the further course. Our companies can be sure that, as long as we have direct or indirect orders to shut down businesses, we will have schemes to compensate companies and help prevent unnecessary bankruptcy. Extensive economic measures have been put in place for business. The broad business compensation scheme has been extended by June. So far, almost 9 billion have been disbursed. kr through the scheme to approximately 34 000 enterprises. Under the Loan Guarantee Facility, 11 billion have been paid. kr, mainly for small and medium-sized enterprises. There is also a system in place in which municipalities can pay aid adapted to local circumstances, for example: to hard hit travel establishments or restaurants. It's now set at 1.8 billion. kr to this scheme. Several of the financial measures are designed to automatically increase the amount of aid as the activity of the economy slows down. Therefore, if the situation worsened during the weeks and months we now go into, support for those affected will be increased. On Friday we presented proposals for a new wage support scheme, which is a follow-up to a request decision from Stortinget. The scheme has been developed in cooperation with the social partners. It is also in line with the government’s strategy for Norway’s way out of the crisis – creating more, including more. The scheme shall assist those who have been hard hit for a long time and have had to permit their employees, those who have a high level of turnover and will need time to recover from the activity. With this scheme, enterprises may withdraw the permits that may prepare for the reopening of the establishment, even if the revenues are not yet in place. In this way, they will be able to be able to deliver their goods and services more quickly on the day the customers are back. In other establishments, there may be a good opportunity to resume training and development leave in the enterprise pending the commencement of the activity. Overall, the scope of economic measures is extensive and is widely fanned. The government will do what is needed to get the best out of this crisis. On the way out of the pandemic, the effort must be shifted towards increased activity and migration. Then the most important contribution we can make to growth in the private sector will be that we reduce the infection and reopen society and phase out public support schemes. In the context of the revised national budget 2021, the Government will present to the Storting a reassessment of the financial instruments. In the future, therefore, we need to be prepared for both good and bad news as a result of uncertainty about vaccine deliveries and the spread of the mutated virus. The overall response and the composition of local, regional and national responses need to be adjusted on an ongoing basis in line with the evolution of the pandemic in Norway. In this work, the priority of the government will be children and young people first, so jobs. If we can keep the R under 1 through March and April, in May we'll be able to take the first steps towards a more normal everyday life. Basic measures such as keeping hands clean, keeping at home in case of disease, keeping away, infective quarantine and isolation should probably be maintained for a long time. All measures need to be adapted and assessed on the basis of an overall assessment of both the positive and the negative effects of the measures. The measures for the protection of infectious diseases shall be balanced and sufficient to provide the necessary infectious effects, but not more restrictive to the population and to the establishments or longer than is necessary. This becomes even more important when the pandemic goes towards the end. The measures should be local rather than national where possible, and the measures should have the lowest possible burden of action. At the same time, it must be overall that the pandemic in Norway is under control. The Directorate of Health and the Institute of Public Health are now working to see how a gradual degression can take place. This is what the government will come back to in the last half of March. I asked to make this statement in order to give the Storting an update on where we are now. So the situation is not clear today. Over the past few weeks, we have seen a sharp spread of the mutated virus variant and increasing hospitalisations. It's serious. We have another steep hill in front of us. But if we can also do that, the goal of a more normal everyday life is within reach. If we can control the infection in March and April, we can start to open up in May. Many municipalities have implemented strict local measures in the last few weeks. We don't know the effect of these yet. Therefore, it is now too early to resort to stricter national measures. Instead, we further reinforce the model of local action. We're going to have to shut down outbreaks locally even faster, even more definitely. If we succeed, there will be no need for new national measures. If we don't succeed, we're going to have to tighten in fast. I have notified what measures will then come, and am asking everyone to prepare. Whether it be local or national, I ask today for a final effort – that together we also go up this hilltop, hoping that it is the very last one, and that this time we finally reach the top and can open society more in May.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e518	The called alternate for Oslo, Kristoffer Robin Haug, has taken the seat. The President will now, according to Stortinget’s Rules of Procedure § 45, open a short comment round limited to one post of up to 5 minutes from each party group and final statements from the Prime Minister.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e525	Thank you to the Prime Minister, who will come to Stortinget and explain. I think that's a wise thing. There have been images of the situation we are in, and the Prime Minister stayed at it at the end. The Health Minister said last summer that we were now on the top of the mountain and were going down, and that going down was as heavy as going up. It was a picture. Now we're going to the new peaks – we're probably more in a Norwegian mountain landscape where we go up and down. I'll be careful to say that we only have one top left when we have this mutated virus. I think it was an unexplained statement the Prime Minister gave, because what she really says is that the situation is very serious – the R-number of 1.3, we can get the 50 in cases ahead – but she will wait another few weeks to see if new measures are needed. There is a very great responsibility on the municipalities with this line that is now being laid up. The municipalities are the advanced position in the defence against the pandemic. I still think it is unclear how the Prime Minister will assess whether we are on the right track for a week or two. It assumes that we also have close contact about it, because it is then about very interventional measures that come on the other side. The picture we can imagine is that we have two very demanding months ahead of us, perhaps the most difficult months until now, where, in the words of the Prime Minister, the numbers of infections can come out of control. I think we should focus more on it than think that in May we're really through, because then there's enough vaccines. This virus has been unpredictable and may still be. That is why there is reason to pay great attention to those who are now affected by this crisis, to the vulnerability of society. The Corona has proved to be a magnifying glass of vulnerability in our society that was there before the Corona – those who lose work, those who see life's work go down, and all those who also struggle with psychic aspects of being at home and not getting into social communities. On Sunday I realized that the main message to the Prime Minister was to say that she was proud of people. I agree. Everyone who has met this crisis has supplied it, but also the sum of people. What we've seen is what the Norwegian community has provided in defense against this pandemic. We have strong hospitals, we have strong and competent care for the elderly, we have municipalities that can take responsibility, and we also have a big deal that can make decisions that are needed. Now there's going to be a commission that's going to give us an assessment, and today we're discussing social security here in Stortinget. I just want to add that a lesson for future preparedness is that we need to invest in upgrading this community, not privatising and breaking it up, not creating gaps in the care of the elderly that allow us to get infected there because we have too poor coverage, bet on a serious working life and not least bet on the social partners – part of the community we have reason to be proud of. We can be proud of people, the question is whether we can be proud of politics in the summary. I still believe that one of the reasons we have received these mutated variants is that we have not had control of the import infection. For too long there was too little control, so that finally a drastic action had to be taken to: close borders. So that's the question of vaccines. There I would say that we from the Labour Party support that we are part of European cooperation. So it's like we're completely open about the agreements, and we've been completely open about the assessments we've made about this. So we have chosen a strategy in which we cooperate with the EU, because that's what gave us the best guarantee that we could have an agreement with many companies, to be sure that we got vaccines when they were done. None of us knew at the time when we had to conclude these agreements, which vaccine would come first, and who would have major production challenges. One of the companies that was early to signal large volumes has not been able to deliver those volumes, that is, AstraZeneca, and that is essentially what makes us lie further behind at the beginning of the vaccination than what we had originally hoped we would be. At the same time, I want to stress that the government has taken these reservations all the time, that it assumes that we do not have challenges with this. So to the question of intensification: the picture given that nothing has been done is basic – basic – wrong. We had 289 intensities last year when we started this pandemic. We now have a capacity that can raise the intensive sites to 1,200. There are insufficient intensities, as it is not possible to do so soon, but there are 3000 staff trained to help in intensive care under the guidance of skilled medical staff. That means that we've made a take-off in health businesses, but when they start it escalating, that's a professional question. And the fact that Ahus within the Great Oslo region has a capacity challenge means that the other hospitals have to step up with their capacity.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e1042	Norway ratified the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities, CRPD, in 2013. Norway is obliged to comply with this Convention, irrespective of how it was implemented in Norwegian law. The Convention has been implemented in Norwegian law in the event of transformation. Changes were made to the legal structure and other legislation to comply with the requirements of the Convention. The government is continuously working to ensure compliance with the CRPD. The precautionary principle helps to ensure that Norwegian law is interpreted and applied in line with Norway’s international obligations, i.e. the CRPD. The Committee monitoring the implementation of the Convention interprets some of the Convention’s obligations extensively. This can help create uncertainty about the content of the Convention and make it less suitable for incorporation into the human rights law. Among other things, the CRPD Committee believes that disability can never provide a basis for depriving any of their legal capacity or for forced health care. In Norway and many other countries, this is allowed when last resort is needed and control mechanisms are available. If the understanding of the Committee is based on the central provisions of Norwegian law, it would be contrary to the Convention. As late as April 30, 2020, Stortinget rejected by a large majority a proposal to incorporate the CRPD into the Human Rights Act. The same reasons that spoke against the incorporation of the CRPD a year ago, in my view, also apply today. The issue of Norway's accession to the CRPD's individual complaint scheme has also been addressed in detail in the past. In the examination of this issue in 2017, the clear majority of the Stortinges found, like the government, that Norway should not join the individual complaint scheme of the CRPD. The justification is related to the broad interpretation of the CRPD Committee, which also applies to the question of membership of the CCR.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e1324	Report. St. 5 for 2020-2021, Social security in an uncertain value, doing good for government policies in social security for the four coming years, and being the second social security major message under the Solberg Government. Previous message, Report. St. 10 for 2016–2017, Risk in a safe society, focused on preparedness. In this message attention will be paid to prevention. On the basis of the challenge picture, the government has done its utmost to promote and promote action within the following seven themes: the covid-19 pandemic, civil-military cooperation and total defence, prevention of national security, digital security, complex threats, prevention and preparedness locally and regionally and prevention, preparedness and rescue services in our most northern maritime areas. In addition, the message contains its own chapter on research and development, on international cooperation and on ongoing and implemented measures taken by the government. I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation in this matter. The setting is almost as long as the message, so there is no doubt that social security is involved. That's very good. It has also been a good commitment from many different hearing agencies that have given valuable input and view of the message. Ensuring the security of citizens is one of the most important tasks of the state. In the last police survey, a total of 94 pst. of us it feels safe to stay and live where we are. Norway is a stable and democratic society with a low level of conflict and a high level of trust between people and between people and governments, which provides a very good starting point for work on social security. The government has contributed to a strong rise in this area in the last seven years. All the recommendations of the Yard Commission are now followed up. Much has been done to protect the country and people from terror. The police have been given an enormous boost, both through 3,600 new police posts and through new and modern equipment and better methods. Several major and important investments have been made, such as a new emergency centre, object protection and the establishment of secure data centres in the justice sector. Police have got new and larger helicopters with better transport capacity. There is an agreement on 24-hour continuous helicopter standby for the police in North Norway. In addition, the police have agreed both with the loco-helicoptera to the Coast Guard and with the Air ambulance on the use of their helicopter resources where necessary. The rescue service is strengthened by 16 new and less weather-dependent rescue helicopters to replace today's Sea King fleet – some are already in place. A new rescue helicopter base will be created in Tromsø to strengthen preparedness in North Norway. In 2020, HF coverage was created in the northernmost seas, up to the North Pole, in order to retain the responsibility of Norway for the rescue response in a highly demanding area of increased maritime traffic. The grid has been developed and used throughout the country, which has meant a great deal to local and regional preparedness. 1 000 organisations and more than 59 000 subscribers from different emergency and emergency services are currently using this important communication tool. Fire training is strengthened by the establishment of a new fire and rescue personnel training school at the Norwegian Fire School in Teldsund, and civil defence has provided additional means of training, basic training, personnel, equipment and maintenance. Measures in the national digital security strategy amount to 1.6 billion. includes competence measures and a new national technical security package to strengthen and further develop capabilities to deal with serious digital attacks. A national cybersecurity centre is established in the NSM, as a focal point for national and international cooperation in the detection, management, analysis and consultation of digital attacks. At Kripos, a private cybercrime centre, a spearhead in the police force has been created to prevent and investigate crime in the digital space. New insurance laws to adapt today's challenge picture have also come into place. In the message, the government gives a good picture of the pitfalls, risks and vulnerabilities that can and probably will also affect us, and at the same time gives a good idea of what measures to put in place in the coming years to meet them. This will take place through both regulatory development, security measures and the further development of knowledge and competences, where the preventive perspective will be a constant basis, in order to strengthen our capacity to protect our values and enhance the resilience of society. In recent years, individual communities and the country as a whole have been facing major preparedness tests. The summer of 2018 brought a long and extensive drought with the following forest and terrain industries. Fluctuations and slippages have caused more communities to lose their lives and to bring great values. I have not least noticed that voluntary efforts are being put forward as an invaluable value when crises occur. The Coronapandemic affects all aspects of society and the way we associate with each other and live our lives. Norway has so far come out of the pandemic, both economically and also in the number of corona-related deaths. For those who have lost one of their loved ones, who stand without a job, are permissive or know long-term loneliness, this is of course a poor comfort, but we are in it, we are far on the way to the same measures, and we have a state that has economic muscles to set up. The coronary apathy has undoubtedly revealed vulnerabilities in health preparedness. This will be taken into account, as well as the construction of storage facilities for infectious diseases and some important medicines and the building-up of intensive capacity are now taking place in the Ministry of Health and Welfare. The loyalty and the upswing that people in Norway have shown in this time is almost unique in a world-class context. Trust also in times of crisis is a value that cannot be valued enough. I would also say that international cooperation under the pandemic has been successful. This applies to the tasks that take the longest time, which are excluded from the statistics. ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e1707	Extensive digitisation characterises societal development and is an important premise for the creation of value added, economic growth and social efficiencies. We share the concern that the digital space provides potential threat actors with the ability to commit data breaches, spy, conduct digital attacks and carry out impact campaigns. We are therefore pleased that the government will give priority to strengthening our digital security nationally and strengthening cooperation internationally. Digital security is becoming an increasingly important part of the State and Social Security field, and a large part of the threat activity against Norway is currently taking place in digital space. The Government’s National Digital Security Strategy provides the basis for making digital Norway safer. Digital security breaches can be prevented if both businesses and individuals contribute to prevention. All establishments are recommended to follow the National Safety Authority’s advice on password and the basic principles of NSM on ICT security. However, we note that despite considerable efforts on educational instruments with appropriate advice and guidance, there is still a lack of understanding and follow-up. The establishment of the NSM National Cybersecurity Centre has facilitated strong professional cooperation between more than 40 private and public enterprises. The national cybersecurity centre is our national response function for serious digital attacks and operates the national digital infrastructure alert system. The NCSC is a focal point for national and international cooperation in the detection, management, analysis and counselling of digital attacks. NC3 was established in 2019 as a department at Kripos and constitutes a spearhead in police prevention and investigation of crime in the digital space. In addition, NC3 is central to police national intelligence production linked to threats in the digital domain. With NCSC in NSM and NC3 in Kripos, the authorities have established two complementary national centres for digital security. Right supports the government’s proposal to establish the Digital Security Act implementing the EU NIS Directive and an assessment of how EU legal requirements can and should be incorporated into Norwegian law to ensure digital security of products and services connected to the Internet. Digital competence is a scarce resource nationally and internationally. The government takes this very seriously and indicates that it has been allocated about 800 million. through the National Digital Security Competence Strategy to strengthen digital security capability in line with societal needs.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e1724	"At present it is the fact that the political heaven, world political, is sloppy to a degree that has not been the case for many years." That is what former Prime Minister Gunnar Knudsen said in February 1914. Just a few months later, the shots fell in Sarajevo, and World War I was a fact. The quotation shows how difficult, not to say impossible, it is to predict how the evolution of the world becomes at a given time. It's the backbone of today's debate, because today's point is to make sure that people in our whole country are safer, and that we can face it unforeseeable – that is, to be prepared and ready. In one area, it is quite obvious that the right-wing government has not thought that we should be prepared, because besides clean water, access to food is the most basic thing for us human beings, in order for us to be able to live. What makes Norway particularly vulnerable to just the lack of food is particularly two aspects of how our country is geographical and our location. First of all, we have very little food soil, and only a very small part of the food soil we have is suitable for producing grain. In addition, Norway is vulnerable because we can be cut off at sea. That's exactly what happened during World War I, with which I opened the post. Norway was a neutral country, but despite the fact that we were not at war, security was challenged for the benefit of a large number of people in this country, and it was a source of famine. One of the most famous pictures from World War I has been taken at the end of Karl Johan's gate in the park at the Castle, where you can see that a guy starts plowing the field with a horse to set potatoes. It reminds us how important it is to use the resources we have when there is a crisis, but also that, as I said, we have people who can do the job. Therefore, the red-green side will provide an overall better common assurance of people in this country. And if we're going to be safe, it doesn't just keep having balls, we also have to have food.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e3456	The government has chosen to promote the social security message at the same time as the long-term defence plan, and that is a good reason. The distinction between war and peace has become more unclear, and we see that in the grey zone between war and peace, there are instruments that can affect across sectors. The 1946 Defence Commission decided that the defence of Norway should be based on both military defence and broad civil protection. The total resources of society were to be mobilised for the defence of the country. But it is also about the defence support for civil society in crisis. The total defense was developed after World War II, but was after the fall of the wall far on the road considered unnecessary. In 2016, the government launched a programme to further develop the overall defence. The programme aims to increase resilience to threats and attacks aimed at societally critical functions and further develop civil authorities’ support for defence. Since 22 July 2011, a number of measures have been put in place to improve the capacity to support civil crisis management. The Special Defence Command and the Marine Hunter Command are in continuous counter-terror alert, permanent helicopter standby is in place to support the police and cooperation between the PST and the Intelligence Service is strengthened. The new 2017 aid guide makes it easier to request support from the Defence Agency and the decisions on assistance from the Defence Agency can be taken more quickly. Until recently, only the defense's support for the police was permitted by law. Following the decision of the Stortinget on 16 February, the defence assistance to the other civil authorities is now also provided for in the Defence Act. Civil support for military forces is essential to our defence capabilities. In recent years, the government has updated the plans for the total defence of Norway. The government prioritises measures to ensure access to food, fuel, transport, health preparedness and power supply in crisis and war. For example, the defence through strategic partners has more than doubled the number of logistic bases in Norway. The defence has e.g. access to Bring's vehicle fleet throughout the country and their cooperation with Norway's largest food wholesalers ensures access to supplies in crisis and war. Practice Trident Juncture was in many ways a test of the ability of civil society to interact with the Defense and on the ability of allied reception. Experience was well under way, but an experience from that exercise is that a new host country support battalion is now being created in the new long-term defence plan. The tragic racial accident in Gjerdrum once again showed the importance of the matching of resources. In crisis and war, it is essential to be able to interact across sectors. [[President clubs]]
ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e3471	That's enough time out.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e3476	Cybersecurity and cyber capacity need to be given increased attention. Our minimum defense is too small to allow the defence over time to enable us to meet our national and international obligations. The Social Security Communication states that there is an increased risk of espionage, digital screening and sabotage, and the dissemination of disinformation and false news. The increased digitisation of society gives state and non-state actors new tools and arenas to pursue their strategic interests. You and I as citizens become more and more dependent on technology and electronic communications both privately and at work. We are more and more exposed to digital attacks. The Central Party requires increased deployment of the Intelligence Service and the Cyber Defense Service, including: by increasing the appropriation of the Defence to 2 pst. of gross domestic product. In addition, in the alternative state budget, we have proposed several ICT study sites. The Communication lacks concrete measures to prevent and prevent the serious consequences of health preparedness, food security, digital vulnerability and communication incidents – in order to strengthen total defence. It is good to put in place a law on digital security, develop competences on digital security and implement national exercises on digital security. The local authorities must also take part in the prevention work, and it will probably support the Central Party rapporteur, Mr Helgestad, in the East of Toten and the professionals at the Commune-CSIRT. But there is more to it: the Central Party wants to look at the establishment of a common, national cloud service. Germany, the Netherlands and France have done everything they can. ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e3489	It is clear that the minority in the Judicial Committee, consisting of the Right and the Advance Party, disregards the essential points raised during the consultation on social security in an uncertain world. It requires that all establishments with exposed objects and infrastructure must themselves assess the risks and ensure adequate safety. According to the hearings, Norway has no full education in this field today. Very few candidates with complete engineering competence are trained in physical security. One possible consequence of this is that design is often outsourced to a professional environment lacking this competence. The result can quickly be insufficient securing and expensive solutions. Defence construction and the national safety authority are the main advisers in Norway for physical security and protection. But some people have to train the consultants who work there, and the fact is that so far a significant proportion of them have come from this professional environment at NTNU. If the environment continues its activity as a national centre, they will be able to further develop the subject area and train candidates who have both been trained to use them and to think completely about safety. Continuing training will also exacerbate today's sorely needed skills in this area. ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e3747	The ILO core conventions provide for minimum rights to be respected in the workplace. They can be divided into four main categories: freedom of organisation and collective bargaining, the prohibition of child labour, the prohibition of forced labour and the prohibition of discrimination. Norway has ratified all eight conventions and is therefore obliged to comply with them. This applies irrespective of how they were implemented in Norwegian law. It is up to each country to decide how this should be done. The Conventions have been implemented in Norwegian law by establishing legal harmonisation or by transformation. The principle of pre-emption, which is based on the assumption that Norwegian law is in conformity with international law, ensures that Norwegian law is interpreted and applied in line with the principles of international law of Norway, including the ILO core conventions. The argument in this case is very similar to the one we had in the case of the CRPD. The question of which international conventions should be incorporated into the Human Rights Act was carefully assessed in the preparatory work of the law. In 1993 the Human Rights Committee made assessments of the ILO Conventions on Combating Forced Labour and on Freedom of Organisation. For various reasons, the Conventions were not considered suitable for incorporation into the Human Rights Act. Nor in the proposition was it proposed to incorporate some of the ILO core conventions. The Ministry drew a distinction between conventions containing rights to a number of relationships which apply to all persons, regardless of age and sex, ethnicity and the like, and conventions which apply either to special groups of persons or to specific rights. The core ILO Conventions fall within the last category of Conventions. The right-hand view in this case is that if one is to open up to several conventions, questions will easily arise as to where the boundary will go. It could also be a perception that conventions incorporated into Norwegian law without priority are considered to be less important or otherwise. As a general rule, the principle that it is only the pronounced general conventions that are to be incorporated into the Law of Human Rights that we want to maintain. In the right, it makes sense to exercise restraint in incorporating conventions into the human rights law because, as part of the law, the conventions are given the step height above Norwegian law. This means that Norwegian legal sources are pushed into the background if there is a need to harmonise provisions that draw in different directions. The problem arises by adhering to legal standards developed elsewhere than in our legal system. We believe that this can represent a democratic problem, especially in view of the loss of control over how our own legislation should be interpreted, as well as the lack of control over who is shaping the interpretation rules. In particular, this will be put at the forefront where the Conventions have their own enforcement bodies, which may be composed of nations and people far from the Norwegian legal tradition. The right will uphold the principle that it is the pronounced general conventions that should be incorporated into the human rights law, and we therefore do not vote in favour of this proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e3794	The ILO’s eight core conventions provide for minimum rights to be respected in the workplace. Norway has ratified the eight conventions and is obliged to comply with them. This applies irrespective of how they were implemented in Norwegian law. The Conventions have been implemented in Norwegian law, either by finding that Norwegian law already complies with the Convention obligations or by its own implementing legislation. For example, in 2000 changes were made to the penal code and the protective duty law in the context of the ratification of the ILO Convention on the Prohibition and Immediate Elimination of the Worst Forms of Child Labour. Furthermore, the presumption principle helps to ensure that Norwegian law is interpreted and applied in line with international obligations, including the ILO core conventions. I would like to stress that the interests of the core ILO Conventions are protected by the Constitution and by human rights conventions that have already been incorporated into the Human Rights Act. This applies, for example: the right to freedom of organisation. The question of which Conventions should be incorporated in preference to the Law on Human Rights has been carefully considered in the past, such as: by the Human Rights Committee, which considered that the ILO Conventions on Freedom of Association and on Combating Forced Labour were not suitable for incorporation into the Human Rights Act. When examining the human rights law, the Judicial Committee stated that it was not appropriate to incorporate all human rights conventions into the human rights law. Both the Government and the Storting have repeatedly stressed that incorporation into the Human Rights Act should be reserved for the pronounced General Conventions. When incorporating the UN Convention on Women’s Rights into the Human Rights Act in 2009, the Ministry stated that inclusion with priority should be reserved for exceptional cases and that ever-increasing incorporation could lead to an opinion that conventions not incorporated into the Human Rights Act are considered to be less important or otherwise. In my view, these principles should be maintained. I also point out that on 16 May 2019, the Stortinget examined a proposal for the incorporation of the ILO’s core conventions into the Human Rights Act. The proposal was not adopted. The same reasons that spoke against incorporation two years ago, in my view, also apply today. The ILO core conventions are important conventions that guarantee workers central rights, but the conventions have already been sufficiently implemented in Norwegian law.

I am very surprised, because it is completely wrong, the impression that someone is trying to create in this case, and also in the previous case. It's not that it's just the political and civil rights that are fundamental human rights. I have to say, I'm pretty worried about that, because it's important to put fundamental rights in place. We're not threatened here in Norway, but it's incredibly important that we get this in place as part of our law. ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e4336	This case concerns an SV representative proposal on the establishment of an expert committee to investigate the possible lasting consequences of the pandemic on travel habits and traffic forecasts in the transport sector. I would like to thank the Committee for its constructive cooperation. The Korona epidemic has resulted in the most invasive measures Norway has had in peacetime. The anti-infectious measures affect the daily lives of all citizens of the country. The transport sector is also heavily affected. It is crucial for the transport sector whether the pandemic will bring about lasting behavioural changes and changes in transport demand. It is the permanent changes that are important when drawing up a national transport plan for the period 2022 to 2033 and which form the basis for large infrastructure investments. NTP must take account of this uncertainty. Several research results are beginning to give us answers on the possible duration of the consequences. Several surveys have been carried out by the Transport Economic Institute to examine the changes in working and travelling habits and whether there is reason to expect the changes to be permanent. Responses from the studies suggest that there is a significant potential for reducing the volume of work travel and for a more even distribution of work travel over the day. Activities shall, on the basis of a response from the Council of State, be under way during the planning period to assess the order, scale and progress of projects in the light of changing assumptions. An expert sample will have limited time if the response is to be provided Stortinget in time for the treatment of NTP, and the sample had to make use of the same statistics and the same early research as that already available to the Ministry of Transport. It is important to note that, in its response to the Committee, the Council of State emphasises the need to facilitate the implementation of investments in national transport plans that allow for an increase in uncertainty, which in turn leads to innovation in planning and the need to meet the transport needs of the population in a cost-effective and climate-friendly manner. In the big cities, there is a lot of money to invest in infrastructure that is used only in the rush time for a few hours a day. It has to be a goal that these peaks are flattened out, which also had saved society too much money. This can also be reinforced in the future if the use of home offices, flexible working hours and digital meetings becomes more common than before. It must be an objective that collective solutions, especially trains, facilitate travel time to be part of working time.

We in the Center Party believe that it should, and that should be a goal for absolutely everyone. An effective harmful trap for large predators is very important. It is important to distinguish between a fall in licence and a fall in damage. The size has been clear that licence and quota felling are carried out by common hunters and shall be the main instrument for controlling the predatory wildlife stock. Harm traps, on the other hand, are about taking out individuals who do harm. The problem is that the harmful traps, those to be effective, those to be prepared, those to do the job in the event of an animal being taken out, must be given an opportunity through the search for a licence to train the necessary knowledge and skills. They must be allowed to use the tools needed in the licence hunt to train, for example. This with a dog. The lot of the centre will point out that the aim of the trap is to extract predators that damage grazing areas and that this is done as quickly as possible to avoid animal diseases. Effective damaging traps are also a central tool to ensure the foreseeable conditions of the grazing industry. We have to trust that there are hunters who can do the job, and we have to trust that it happens quickly. It's exactly the same as when you drive on an animal on the road, when you're going to kill animals that are injured. Then you need professional people, and they need to use the tool that's needed. Even though it is said that ordinary hunting methods are to be used, today there are many ways to do this more effectively, especially when it comes to finding the animal and knowing where it is. Whether all these means have been a dialogue over time, as the Council of State says, but these are instruments that need to be systematised and that people need to access. I myself have been sitting as rapporteur in a municipality with a major predatory problem. I've been in charge of the National Park Board both in Rondane and on Dovrefjell, and I've been away with all kinds of predators. The only way to do this is to make sure that it happens quickly, that you have an effective withdrawal, and that you have people who know their job. They must be allowed to practise in the license hunt.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e4873	Emissions in the world must be halved in 10 years in order to achieve the objectives of the Paris Agreement. In a world that cuts emissions, we are at risk of high-emission economies and enterprises becoming less and less competitive. The cost of not acting can also be significant. Companies need to implement new technologies and solutions that replace yesterday's fossil solutions. Product and services must have much lower climate footprints in the future; investing in green solutions must pay off. A green competitiveness strategy was presented by the government in October 2017. It helps to create a framework for the green conversion in Norway. A Green Competitiveness Expert Selection recommended the work on the strategy. The selection received input from central actors in the community. Part of the recordings came in the form of a Green Competitiveness Roadmap. Many road maps describe how greenhouse gas emissions can be cut to zero by 2050, while industry can increase value creation and new jobs. The 2017 strategy focused in particular on the following areas: establishing a market for green solutions green and innovative public procurement research, innovation and technology development; green solution infrastructure climate risk and funding circular economy increasing export of green solutions I'm getting closer to the follow-up of these areas here today. Green competitiveness is an integral part of government climate policy. The climate plan, as we presented in January, is therefore a key element in green conversion and green competitiveness. The climate plan proposes to achieve the climate targets while at the same time facilitating more jobs, enhanced well-being and sustainable growth in the Norwegian economy. The government has said that we want to increase the CO 2 tax and make it more expensive to pollute. It makes it more profitable to cut emissions, it makes it easier to plan emission reductions and more profitable to invest green. Governments will continue to invest in research and innovation, both in Norway and through participation in the EU research programme through Horizon Europe. Research and innovation are contributing to the development of new green solutions that can increase the pace of migration. In the climate plan, the government will strengthen Enova’s climate change instrument for innovation. It is particularly relevant in the context of emission allowances, as public support for research and development is an important complement to the allowances system. The Green Platform cooperation is the new measure taken by the government to coordinate, strengthen and target efforts to stimulate low-emission development and green competitiveness in Norwegian business. The management will focus on CO 2 capture and storage. The government will implement Longship, a project for just capturing, transport and storage. Long-distance shipping shall contribute to the development of CO 2 management as an effective climate action and provide technology development from an international perspective. The finance industry was one of the industries that developed a road map already in 2016. Work on green finance has slowed down in recent years, and much has been done both in the management and in the private sector. In order to succeed in the green transition, it is absolutely crucial that both public and private capital go to the green and forward-looking solutions. As stated in the Climate Action Communication, there is a good dialogue with the business community on green finance in the future. The government will have a dialogue on how we can get better reporting from the company on climate and environment, and we can use it to make good investment decisions. It will be exciting to follow along in time. The industry has continued its road map work in Process21. We also strengthen green competitiveness by providing good tools for maritime nutrition. We use public funds in the Swedish Research Council, Innovation Norway and Enova to both provide grants and loans, triggering private investments for future-oriented and competitive low- and zero-emission solutions. We are aware that, although we get a lot in green shipping, the work on increasing green competitiveness in the maritime sector is a work that will go on for years. We're in the starting range. ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e5088	There weren't many people in this room. There are many opinions about the medical plans in Oslo. The plans are controversial in the trade unions, among the citizens of Oslo and among political parties, both in Stortinget and in Oslo City Council. In 2016, the board of Health South-East decided to lay down Ullevål Hospital and build a new, large regional hospital on Gaustad, as well as a local hospital on Aker. The plans were approved by Health Minister Bent Höie, who is sitting here in the hall. The plans have met with strong criticism. Several believe that the planned hospitals will be too small for a growing population, while the professional community has feared patient safety. The trustees of the employees have wanted Ullevål to be expanded, rather than the solution pointed out by the board. It was then a desire and a pressure to light Ullevål as an alternative, and the board of Health South-East presented its lighting in May 2019. It concluded that it would cost close to 13 billion. kr more and delay the project by seven years if the new hospital was added to Ullevål in front of Gaustad. The lighting has received criticism from several teams, and several have wanted a full investigation of the Ullevål option. The State budget for 2020 provided a loan guarantee of 30 billion. kr to Health South-East for the expansion plans at Gaustad and Aker. The new hospital buildings will therefore constitute Norway’s largest land-based construction project. As a general rule, the upper elevation limit in Oslo is 42 metres, while Oslo University Hospital is now planning a 76-metre-high hospital in connection with the Rikshospitalet in Gaustad. Health South-East's proposal for a regulatory plan was also made last year, when the dialogue with the Municipality of Oslo did not take place. The local politicians of Oslo have clearly demonstrated their opposition to the hospital plans at Gaustad. If you succeed in caring for the needs of the citizens of Oslo for hospital services, it would be beneficial for a broad political majority to support the solution chosen. As the matter stands now, it seems that such ambition is not real. The opposition to the establishment of a new hospital in Gaustad is increasing in strength, and the arguments for expanding the capacity of existing Ullevål hospitals are increasing. Winsnes and Civil Economist and State Suites Per H. Christiansen, brings up several interesting perspectives. The critics who already in the planning phase warned that the hospital would be too small were right. Thousands of patients were in the corridor already in the first year. The Prestitial Hospital, which was planned for zero Corridor Asians, has had major problems since the opening. The Kalnes project has been described as scandalous by many. It turned out that the hospital was undersized in most functional areas. Despite Kalnes being one of Health South-East's most modern hospitals, the hospital is crowded, which means that the work flow in the hospital is hindered and the operation becomes less efficient. At the same time, the hospital has a demanding economy. If health south-east is to be economically sustainable in addressing growing needs, in particular after 2032, current projects must be planned for good and efficient operation. It's not the same thing as minimizing the functional areas. The criticism of the New OUS plans is much the same — too small bed capacity and lack of and too small functional areas. Two out of three patients treated at the OUS do not live in Oslo. The hospitals around Oslo are largely dependent on the OUS, both as a rear guard and to deal with the most serious injured and ill patients from all over the region and in many cases from all over the country. According to this standard, an alternative draft plan is to be drawn up if the height of the building exceeds 42 metres. Health South-East has therefore also developed a plan with a development solution within 42 metres of the height of Gaustad. Aker Hospital, on the other hand, is planned for higher construction. The regulatory plan proposes a construction height of 71.2 metres. Also for Aker Hospital, an alternative plan has been developed that shows an extension solution with a height of 42 metres. In both hospitals, helicopter platforms are planned to exceed the building height by 4 to 6 metres. Central themes in this dialogue have been the exploitation of the land concerned, height, protected construction, green structure and the interaction between the new building mass and the existing building mass. There are few projects that have been investigated to the same extent as New Oslo University Hospital. The planning has been over a long period of time and has been comprehensive and costly. The target picture with a new structure was adopted almost five years ago and additional investigations have been carried out to support the agreed hospital structure. A number of adjustments have been made to the projects based on input from trustees and the trade unions at Oslo University Hospital. Now the pre-projection work is under way. 40 participation groups have been established as an essential part of the pre-projection work. Each group consists of eight to ten employees, users, security services and trustees. Over many years, Oslo University Hospital has spent considerable resources on maintenance and investment in buildings that are not suitable for future hospital operations. Many buildings are operated with temporary permits from different supervisory authorities. Progress in the project plans is now important in order to achieve more targeted investment in Oslo. Users are quite clear that they cannot accept a postponement of the project.
