ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e5251	This crisis should overshadow everything we do in health policy. The coronary apathy is small in relation to antibiotic resistance. Antibiotic resistance is called the health sector’s climate crisis. Every year, 750,000 people die as a result of antibiotic resistance. Forecasts show that worldwide, 10 million people will die annually from antibiotic-resistant bacteria from 2050. Antibiotics are the basic plank of the health care system. Without effective antibiotics, much of the health sector will disappear. It is essential for almost all operations, from cancer treatment to cesarean section. This matter should be important to everyone, but to me it is personal. I got two types of broad spectrum antibiotics when I had sepsis. One of them didn't work. If the other one hadn't worked, I wouldn't be here today. If we don't deal with this now, our children and grandchildren will ask us why we didn't act when we had time. The most important measure Norway can take is the use of less antibiotics and, in particular, broad-spectrum antibiotics. Norwegian doctors are withholding antibiotics, but we're not the best in class. Before the coronary pandemic, Norwegian doctors distributed significantly more antibiotics than their Swedish colleagues, and up to 30 pst. more than its colleagues in the Netherlands. The aim was to reduce the consumption of so-called wide-spectrum antibiotics in the population by 30 pst. by the end of 2020. In May 2020, I asked the Council of State how we were going, and it was clear that Norway would not reach the target. Now it looks like we can reach the goal. The closure in order to counteract coronary infection, improved hand hygiene and less physical contact has led to a reduction in infection from other viruses and bacteria. I'm still worried that less use will just be a bluff, and that it will be used more when society opens up again. What will the Council of State do to ensure that the use of antibiotics does not increase again when the pandemic is over? In the budget, SV proposed earmarked funds for antibiotic resistance and better diagnostic systems. We didn't get a majority for that. In the United Kingdom, they work a lot to get faster and better diagnostic systems. Then they can quickly find the right antibiotics, or find out that the person should not be given antibiotics. It will significantly reduce the use of antibiotics. Does the government have work to do to improve and speed up diagnostic systems? When patients need antibiotics, it is much better to use narrow-spectrum antibiotics. We have had problems getting some of the narrow spectres on the market. Private companies make less money when they sell generic medicines, like penicillin. SV has made proposals on the state production of medicinal products, StatMed. We haven't had a majority for that. We had a majority to investigate Norwegian production of antibiotics, but the government parties voted against it. When will the Stortinge get the case we are asking for, and does the government have concrete solutions and agreements to produce narrow-spectrum antibiotics in Norway? New antibiotics will cost you dearly, and the whole plan is to put them on the shelf and not use them. If we use new antibiotics, resistance will increase, and then we'll be on the starting line again. Therefore, private companies cannot profit from selling new broad spectrum antibiotics. It clearly shows that capitalist thinking does not work. Without the community, the state, we can't get new antibiotics and at the same time have less antibiotic resistance. We have two funding mechanisms called push mechanisms and pull mechanisms – one to finance research and one for production and safety for those who will try to produce new antibiotics. The UK health service starts these days a pilot for such a ‘pull mechanism’ where they will pay for the value of new antibiotics to society. Is Norway involved in a conversation with the United Kingdom and others on both pull and push mechanisms and does the government have any solutions for them? Antibiotics are almost like carpet bombing the body. It kills the «bad» bacteria, but also the «good» bacteria. We know that it's a better medicine that just takes the bad ones, and it's bacteriophages. The knowledge of bacteriophages has been given a new spring, and new research shows that bacteria that become immune to antibiotics are becoming more open to bacteriophages. An attack on two fronts could be the rescue we need. The problem is that one type of bacteriopathic disease only attacks one type of bacterium. Since there are so many bacteria, we need a lot of bacteriophages in stock to treat the diseases. In Paris and Stockholm, there is a lot of research in bacteriology. What does the government do in terms of both research and cooperation with other countries to use bacteriota in the treatment of bacteria? We had a majority on Stortingen to reduce the import of antibiotic resistance. Three important decisions were: to warn patients who are planning to use non-North healthcare providers, since we know that many of these people will travel home with bacteria that are immune to antibiotics to require prior authorisation for reimbursement of hospital expenses abroad; reducing the increased use of patient treatment in countries with a high risk of antibiotic resistance; The Right Party, the Advance Party and the Left Party annulled the Decision. Much of the increase in antibiotic resistance is due to the use of antibiotics in agriculture — not in Norway, but in other countries. Worldwide, we use more antibiotics for meat breeding than for sick people who need it. When we import meat from farms that feed animals with antibiotics, our money is used to increase the incidence of antibiotic resistance. I have asked the Minister of Agriculture to reduce imports, but it is impossible with current EEA rules. That is why we have launched a plan b, namely the labelling of meat products, so that consumers can choose products that do not contribute to antibiotic resistance. But the right-wing parties voted against the proposal. I think we need a radical government that works against the import of bacteria that are immune to antibiotics. However, I hope that the Minister of Health will respond clearly with concrete measures in Norway to reduce the prevalence of antibiotic resistance.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e5299	I share the representative's commitment to antibiotic resistance. Just tonight it looks like only the two of us are engaged, but I know that many others in the Storting are also engaged in this matter. Antibiotic resistance is a rapidly growing problem in the world and poses a serious threat to our global health. The fight against resistant microbes is a global challenge and international cooperation is a prerequisite for reducing resistance and deaths associated with antibiotic resistance. Resistant microbes are a problem for patients, for healthcare, for public health, for agriculture and the environment. Without effective antibiotics, we will in the future see increased mortality rates of diseases that are currently easy to treat. The government's efforts against antibiotic resistance have produced results. In 2015, we set a very ambitious target for antibiotic use. In both health and agriculture, significant efforts have been made to reduce and maintain low antibiotic consumption, and we have been able to further reduce antibiotic use in recent years. Use in the population should be reduced by 30 pst. by the end of 2020. Preliminary figures indicate that we have achieved this goal and most of the other targets we set in 2015. The target of 20 pst. reduction in antibiotic use in respiratory infections has already been achieved, and the consumption of antibiotics for Norwegian teenagers is now under one third of what it was in 2019. We have also managed to keep the proportion of resistant yellow staphylococci in both hospitals and primary health services very low. In order to measure antibiotic use in hospitals, we have introduced annual measurements that also show a steady decline in recorded infections. In particular, urinary tract infections have decreased. This is important in order to reduce antibiotic use. The government's ambition to reduce antibiotic consumption in agriculture has also been a success. Narasin used in chicken production was phased out in 2016 and the target of reducing the antibiotic consumption of pet animals and food-producing terrestrial animals was achieved as early as 2017. The government’s strategy has been successful, and Norway is still among the leading countries in the world with low antibiotic consumption. In 2020, the Government decided that the Ministry of Health and Welfare, the Ministry of Industry and Fisheries, the Ministry of Climate and Environment, the Ministry of Agriculture and Food and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs will cooperate to further develop the national strategy from 2021. The task of the Public Health Institute is to lead a cross-sectoral expert group updating the professional basis of the strategy. Research and development on new antibiotics and international cooperation are central to the government’s strategy to combat the problem of resistance. Norway has contributed to the development of new antibiotics, primarily through participation in EU cooperation on antimicrobial resistance and health-related infections, where Norway has managed a work package on research and innovation. Incentives developed shall stimulate new antibiotic innovation and at the same time provide us with predictable access to antibiotics. Because when fewer and fewer countries stop using narrow-spectrum antibiotics, there will be fewer producers and a more vulnerable supply chain. The government has therefore been committed to ensuring supply, so that the Norwegian population has access to these central drugs. Interpellant Wilkinson is particularly concerned with the development of new antibiotics. New antibiotics and bacteriophages can provide us with useful tools that we can use for some types of infections or forms of resistance. But that unfortunately doesn't give us any confidence in the problem of resistance. It requires long-term, targeted efforts to take care of the types of antibiotics that we already have. In order to ensure access to older antibiotics, the Health Directorate recommended in February 2020 to take a closer look at the possibility of producing narrow spectrum antibiotics in Norway. We have commissioned the directorate to conduct an opportunity study by the end of 2021. The study should include, inter alia: identify national and international market conditions and assess how the production of antibiotics can be established, financed and organised in order to strengthen drug preparedness. Furthermore, conditions for sustainable production and opportunities for cooperation with other Nordic and European countries and with relevant international organisations shall be considered. We will continue the monitoring of resistant microbes in order to target interventions and research on the effectiveness of interventions. This monitoring continues to require cross-sectoral cooperation with several sectors. Technology and monitoring systems that have been further developed during the coronary apathy, such as: the sequencing capacity may generate new knowledge of the spread and presence of resistant microbes. This will also capture resistant microbes resulting from import infection. The government's ambition is to continue to be an important player in international cooperation on fundamental research, applied research and innovation. We continue to support international initiatives in the fight against antibiotic resistance, such as: Joint Programming Initiative on AMR, where Norway participates and contributes funds. We also provide funds for several programmes in the Research Council that can finance research related to antibiotic resistance. The government strengthens health preparedness through the development of new diagnostic aids, new antibiotics and alternative treatment strategies, as well as improving the security of supply of critical medicinal products. We live longer. There are more chronic diseases and more immunocompromised, more undergoing surgery, and thus more likely to develop infections requiring antibiotic treatment. These still need to be guaranteed good treatment opportunities. Norway is in a special position internationally, and we have managed to keep a low level of resistant microbes. The government's ambition is that we should continue to maintain this favorable position. The coronary apathy has shown us that preparedness for new infectious diseases must be high on the agenda in the future as well. Travel activity will resume after the coronary apathy and lead to an increased risk of importing resistant microbes. The systematic work of the government against antibiotic resistance has produced good results in the health sector, in agriculture and in the farming industry and together an important boost to climate and public health. I am pleased that there is broad political support for cross-sectoral efforts to achieve the objectives of this important area, both nationally and internationally.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-03-09.ana.ud579e5354	It's not because of the coronary apathy that we achieve goals of reducing the use of antibiotics. This is because systematic health care work has been carried out over a long period of time before the pandemic hit, which is a result of the plans undertaken by the government in this area. It is important to underline this because it is just one of the lessons that other countries can learn from Norway, for example. that we have worked systematically against the decision-makers and with the decision-makers, so that they have received concrete guidance from other colleagues on how to reduce antibiotic use in response to patients. We have run population-oriented campaigns, so that when people come to the firm office and settle for example. says that in this situation I don't want to use antibiotics, you actually accept that answer. Not only do you accept that, we've also been told that people are now asking themselves if it's right to use antibiotics when the doctor recommends it, because the population is involved in this work. The same applies to the systematic work done in hospitals and hospitals. That's what has reduced antibiotic use. We see the same thing in agriculture. It's a systematic work. The fact that we in Norway have been willing to say that the veterinarians in agriculture are not allowed to sell medicines is of enormous importance for us to have had lower antibiotic use than many comparable countries where the veterinarians working in agriculture have an economic interest in selling antibiotics. There are models we're trying to get other countries to use. Our international commitment to this work is far from the puzzles. We are one of those countries that, together with the United Kingdom and the Conservative Government there, who have prioritised this area, have joined forces internationally to lift the fight against antibiotic resistance. It was we, health ministers from a few countries, who took the initiative to lift the threat of antibiotic resistance as a separate topic at the UN High Level Meeting to get the Heads of State involved in this issue. It was an important work that has helped to bring the World Health Organisation, together with the World Organisation for Animal Health, the World Food Organisation and the World Environment Organisations, together in this work. Norway contributes significantly financially to the coordinated work, which is precisely based on the same thinking that we have had in Norway, and which I also believe the representative agrees with, namely that this must be a cross-sectoral work. This is why we are contributing, for example: to reduce the release of antibiotics into the wild in India, to take one example.
